# Chapter Forty-Six: The Weight of Every Answer

Morning never truly arrived.

Heavy clouds buried the sun beneath a ceiling of gray, leaving Blackwood Academy wrapped in permanent twilight.

The old bell never rang.

It didn't need to.

Everyone was already awake.

No one slept deeply anymore.

...

Professor Helena made another round through the infirmary before dawn.

She stopped beside Aidan's bed.

Empty.

Again.

She wasn't surprised.

She simply sighed before folding the fresh bandages she had brought.

"You'll come here eventually," she whispered to no one.

...

She found him exactly where she expected.

The eastern wall.

Hands resting on cold stone.

Eyes fixed on the endless forest.

"You've reopened your shoulder."

"I know."

"You tore your side again."

"I know."

"You still haven't finished healing from the bridge."

"I know."

Helena stepped beside him.

"When was the last time you woke up without something hurting?"

Aidan searched his memory.

Nothing came.

"I don't remember."

That answer hurt Helena more than any wound she had stitched.

...

Below them...

Students changed patrol shifts with practiced precision.

Nobody complained.

New first-years learned barrier rotations before breakfast.

Older students silently reinforced damaged walls.

The academy had changed.

It had survived.

But it no longer felt young.

War had stolen that.

...

Aidan's attention drifted.

A wheel squeaked somewhere beyond the southern gate.

A horse breathed heavily.

Someone coughed.

Farther still...

Metal struck stone.

Once.

Twice.

Three times.

His head turned immediately.

"South."

Rowan appeared beside him almost instantly.

"What is it?"

"I don't know."

He hated saying those words.

"But something's there."

Without another question...

Rowan sent scouts.

...

They returned an hour later.

"There was nobody."

Malik looked confused.

"Then what did you hear?"

The lead scout hesitated.

"We did find this."

He handed Rowan a broken iron chisel lying beside fresh rock fragments.

Someone had been cutting stone.

Recently.

But whoever had done it had disappeared before the scouts arrived.

No footprints.

No camp.

Nothing.

Only another unanswered question.

Aidan quietly looked toward the southern hills.

He had heard someone.

He was certain.

Yet certainty no longer guaranteed answers.

...

That afternoon...

The academy received another messenger.

Not from a village.

From one of Blackwood's own patrols.

The message contained only six words.

**We found something beneath the cliffs.**

Nothing more.

No explanation.

No location beyond a rough map.

Rowan immediately assembled the inner circle.

"We move together."

No one suggested otherwise.

...

The cliffs overlooked an ancient valley where fog clung stubbornly to the ground.

Aidan slowed almost immediately.

His hearing stretched through the mist.

Water.

Wind.

Loose stone.

Then...

Breathing.

Several.

Far below.

He raised one hand.

Everyone stopped.

Cassian whispered,

"How many?"

Aidan listened.

The breaths faded.

Then returned.

No rhythm.

No certainty.

"I..."

He frowned.

"I can't tell."

For the first time...

His senses failed him.

Not because they were weak.

Because whatever waited below understood how to remain hidden.

The realization sent a chill through him.

They descended carefully.

Every step tested before weight followed.

The patrol had been right.

Someone had found something.

An enormous section of cliff had collapsed weeks earlier.

Behind it...

A passage.

Old.

Far older than Blackwood.

No markings identified it.

No records mentioned it.

Only darkness stretched inward.

Aidan knelt beside the entrance.

Dust.

Broken stone.

Then...

A faint smell.

Not blood.

Not decay.

Something ancient.

Something he couldn't place.

His stomach tightened.

The sensation vanished before he understood it.

"Anything?" Rowan asked.

Aidan slowly stood.

"Only questions."

---

# Chapter Forty-Seven: The Door That Shouldn't Exist

The passage descended in complete silence.

Not the ordinary silence of abandoned places.

Something deeper.

As though sound itself hesitated.

Even Malik's limp echoed less than it should have.

Lyra quietly moved closer to Aidan.

"You feel it too."

It wasn't a question.

He nodded once.

The tunnel twisted naturally through the mountain until it opened into a circular chamber unlike anything Blackwood had uncovered before.

The walls were smooth.

Too smooth.

No tool marks.

No cracks.

No fallen stone.

Only age.

At the center stood a single stone doorway.

No door.

Only an empty frame carved directly into the rock.

Nothing existed beyond it.

Just solid mountain.

Cassian slowly circled the structure.

"What is this place?"

No one answered.

Because no one knew.

Aidan stepped closer.

Not carelessly.

Drawn by something he couldn't explain.

His hearing caught nothing.

His smell found nothing unusual.

Even his instincts remained strangely quiet.

That frightened him most.

He reached toward the stone.

Before his fingers touched it—

Pain exploded through his chest.

The second heartbeat struck so hard it stole his breath.

THUMP.

He staggered backward.

Lyra caught him before he fell.

"Aidan!"

"I'm alright."

He wasn't.

His heartbeat slowly steadied.

The second one lingered.

Then faded.

Helena knelt beside the stone frame.

"No dust."

Rowan frowned.

"What?"

"The floor."

She pointed.

Everywhere else the chamber held centuries of settled dust.

Except directly before the doorway.

As though...

Someone had stood there.

Recently.

No footprints remained.

No evidence.

Only absence.

Aidan looked at the empty frame again.

The feeling returned.

Not recognition.

Expectation.

As though the doorway itself had been waiting.

For what...

He had no idea.

He took one step backward.

"We leave."

Cassian looked surprised.

"Without searching farther?"

Aidan never looked away from the empty frame.

"Yes."

No one argued.

They trusted him.

The group retreated through the passage.

No one noticed that after the last lantern disappeared around the bend...

A single pebble quietly rolled across the chamber floor.

Then another.

The empty doorway remained exactly as it had before.

Silent.

Waiting.

And somewhere outside...

Before Blackwood's defenders had even reached the academy again...

Another emergency bell began ringing.
:::

# Chapter Forty-Eight: The Bell Before Dawn

The emergency bell did not stop.

One strike.

Then another.

Then another.

Not frantic.

Measured.

Urgent.

The sound followed Blackwood's defenders all the way back through the academy gates.

Students were already evacuating toward reinforced shelters.

Healers hurried stretchers across the courtyard.

No one panicked anymore.

They had learned how to survive fear.

Rowan intercepted the first messenger.

"What happened?"

The young guard struggled to catch his breath.

"The western reservoir..."

He swallowed.

"...it's breaking."

Helena's face hardened.

"The reservoir?"

"If it goes..."

Elias looked toward the academy map.

"...half the lower district floods."

No hunters.

No monsters.

No visible enemy.

Only another disaster demanding every remaining ounce of strength.

...

The western reservoir had been carved into the mountainside generations before Blackwood Academy existed.

Now...

A jagged fracture split its ancient retaining wall.

Water forced itself through the crack with increasing pressure.

Engineers shouted over one another.

"If this section fails—"

"It all goes!"

Aidan climbed the damaged wall without waiting for permission.

His ribs protested immediately.

The wound across his side burned beneath fresh bandages.

He ignored both.

He pressed one hand against the fractured stone.

The crack continued spreading beneath his fingertips.

"This isn't natural."

Rowan looked up.

"What?"

"The pressure."

Aidan closed his eyes.

The stone vibrated differently along different sections.

One place...

The wall sounded hollow.

He struck it once with the butt of his staff.

The sound echoed far deeper than solid stone should have.

"There."

Malik and Cassian immediately moved beside him.

Together they cleared away several loose blocks.

Behind them...

An empty cavity stretched into darkness.

Not ancient erosion.

Something had been dug.

Recently.

No tracks remained.

No tools.

Only another tunnel disappearing beneath the mountain.

Elias looked down into the opening.

"They weakened it from underneath."

No one answered.

There was nothing to add.

The reservoir had not simply begun failing.

Someone...

Or something...

Had made sure it would.

...

Water burst through another section.

The wall lurched violently.

Students cried out below.

Aidan raised both hands.

"Hold."

Crimson light spread across fractured stone.

The crack stopped.

Only barely.

The pressure drove pain through his chest.

His old shoulder injury flared.

Blood trickled from the corner of his mouth.

Still...

He held.

Malik organized workers into evacuation lines.

Lyra directed frightened families away from the valley below.

Cassian descended into the newly exposed tunnel with two instructors.

Not to chase.

To make sure nothing remained beneath them.

Minutes stretched into an hour.

Every heartbeat hurt.

The strain never eased.

Finally...

Engineers reinforced the damaged section with fresh support pillars.

Rowan rested one hand against Aidan's shoulder.

"You can let go."

The Law dissolved.

Aidan staggered backward.

His legs nearly folded beneath him.

He caught himself against broken stone.

The wall stood.

The reservoir held.

Another disaster survived.

Another price paid.

...

Cassian climbed back from the tunnel.

"Nothing."

"No one?"

"No one."

Only silence.

Always silence.

---

# Chapter Forty-Nine: What Silence Takes

The infirmary smelled of herbs.

Clean bandages.

Fresh rain.

And blood.

Too much blood.

Helena silently cleaned the dried crimson from Aidan's side.

"You tore every stitch."

"I know."

"You've reopened your shoulder."

"I know."

"You've strained the fractures in your ribs again."

"I know."

She finally stopped.

Her hands rested motionless.

"You never ask if you're getting better anymore."

Aidan looked toward the ceiling.

"I don't think that's the question anymore."

The words settled heavily between them.

...

He left before dawn.

As always.

The academy remained asleep.

Or pretending to be.

Aidan crossed the courtyard so quietly that the guards on the western wall never noticed him.

His footsteps no longer announced him.

He rarely noticed that anymore.

The old oak waited beneath a sky without stars.

Clouds hid everything.

He rested one hand against the scarred bark.

Closed his eyes.

Listened.

The academy.

The wind.

Distant rivers.

The forest.

Then...

Nothing.

Again.

That unnatural silence.

Not empty.

Watching.

His breathing slowed.

The second heartbeat answered beneath his own.

THUMP.

Another heartbeat echoed somewhere beyond the northern trees.

Then it vanished.

Not retreating.

Simply...

Gone.

As if whoever had been there had realized Aidan was listening.

He opened his eyes.

Someone else spoke.

Not from the forest.

From behind him.

"You left before breakfast."

Lyra.

He hadn't heard her approach.

She carried two wrapped pieces of bread.

She held one toward him.

"You'll forget again."

He accepted it.

This time...

He actually ate.

Slowly.

Mechanically.

She watched until the last bite disappeared.

Only then did she smile.

"There."

He looked at the empty cloth in his hand.

"I almost forgot."

"I know."

She hesitated.

"You don't have to remember everything alone."

He wanted to answer.

Instead...

His hearing caught another distant sound.

A horn.

Far away.

South this time.

Only one blast.

Then silence.

He and Lyra looked toward the horizon together.

Neither spoke.

They didn't need to.

Another burden had already begun.

Before the wounds from the reservoir could close...

Before the exhaustion could ease...

Before breakfast had even settled...

Blackwood would answer another call.

Aidan quietly adjusted the fresh bandages beneath his coat.

The movement reopened one of the smaller wounds across his side.

He didn't even flinch anymore.

He simply turned toward the southern gate.

And started walking.

Lyra fell into step beside him.

Neither asked whether they were ready.

Readiness had stopped mattering a long time ago.

# Chapter Fifty: The Horn in the South

The horn never sounded a second time.

One blast.

Then silence.

Somehow...

That frightened Aidan more.

He and Lyra reached the southern gate before the guards finished opening it.

Rowan, Cassian, Malik, Elias, and Helena were already there.

No one asked whether Aidan intended to come.

That question had disappeared years ago.

The messenger waiting beyond the gate struggled to remain standing.

His clothes were soaked.

Not from rain.

From river water.

"They took..."

He fought for breath.

"...the ferry."

"The southern crossing?" Rowan asked.

The man nodded weakly.

"No one can cross."

"How many?"

"I don't know."

"Were they attacked?"

"I..."

The ferryman looked lost.

"I never saw anyone."

The words settled over the group.

Again.

No witnesses.

No certainty.

Only consequences.

...

The southern ferry crossed the widest section of the Black River.

Without it...

Five villages were effectively cut off from Blackwood.

Food.

Medicine.

Healers.

Everything stopped.

Aidan looked toward the map Elias quickly unfolded.

"If they wanted to destroy it..."

"They would have burned it," Cassian finished.

"They want us delayed," Rowan said.

"They always do."

...

The journey south took most of the morning.

No voices carried through the trees.

No birds.

Only the steady rhythm of boots against damp earth.

Halfway there...

Aidan stopped.

His head turned toward the ridgeline.

"What?"

Malik asked.

He listened.

Very faint.

Wood creaking.

Then...

Splashing.

Not one splash.

Several.

"They're already on the river."

No one else heard it.

They increased their pace.

...

The ferry remained tied to its dock.

Untouched.

No signs of battle.

The rope hadn't even been cut.

The ferryman's hut stood open.

Breakfast still rested on the table.

Cold.

Hours old.

Helena slowly looked around.

"No blood."

Cassian pushed open another door.

"Nothing."

Aidan stepped onto the ferry itself.

The wooden deck groaned beneath his weight.

His eyes drifted toward the far bank.

Then farther.

Mist rolled across the river.

Beyond it...

He thought he saw movement.

A shadow.

Gone before he focused.

He frowned.

Too far.

Or perhaps...

Never there.

His hearing reached across the water.

The river swallowed most sounds.

Then...

A child cried.

Once.

Very faint.

Across the river.

Lyra immediately looked toward him.

"You heard it."

He nodded.

"Yes."

She listened.

"I didn't."

The cry came again.

Then...

Stopped.

No splash.

No footsteps.

Nothing.

Aidan slowly backed away from the ferry.

"We're leaving."

Cassian trusted him immediately.

No one stepped aboard.

Minutes later...

The ferry drifted away from the dock on its own.

No wind.

No current strong enough to break the mooring.

The rope...

Had been cut from beneath the water.

The empty vessel floated into the center of the river.

Then exploded upward as hidden supports beneath it collapsed.

The river swallowed the wreckage.

No one spoke.

Had they boarded...

No one would have reached the opposite shore alive.

...

On the far bank...

Something moved behind the mist.

Only once.

Then vanished.

Aidan stared until his eyes hurt.

Nothing returned.

---

# Chapter Fifty-One: The Burden That Waited

Blackwood had barely returned from the river when another rider arrived.

His horse stumbled through the gate, foam covering its neck.

The rider slid to the ground before anyone reached him.

"They're sick."

"Who?" Helena asked.

"The children..."

He coughed violently.

"...Stonebrook."

Helena's expression changed immediately.

"Sick how?"

"I don't know."

"They just..."

He swallowed hard.

"...stopped waking up."

No hunters.

No claws.

No ambush.

Only another village needing help.

Again.

Aidan looked toward Helena.

"I'll go."

She shook her head immediately.

"No."

"They need you."

"So do you."

His side throbbed beneath fresh bandages.

His shoulder had stiffened after the reservoir.

His ribs still shifted painfully whenever he breathed too deeply.

He ignored all of it.

"I can still move."

Helena looked into his tired eyes.

"I know."

That answer hurt more than refusing him would have.

...

Stonebrook lay east of Blackwood.

The children still breathed.

Their hearts remained steady.

They simply...

Wouldn't wake.

Helena moved from bed to bed.

Nothing explained it.

No fever.

No poison she recognized.

No visible wounds.

Parents stood helplessly beside sleeping sons and daughters.

Aidan quietly walked through the village.

Listening.

The silence felt wrong.

Not because no one spoke.

Because every sleeping child breathed in almost the same rhythm.

He stopped outside the final house.

His head tilted.

One heartbeat.

Different.

He entered.

A little boy lay asleep beside the window.

Unlike the others...

His breathing wasn't steady.

It was slowing.

"Aidan?"

Helena looked up.

"What is it?"

He knelt beside the bed.

"I don't know."

His hand hovered over the child's wrist.

Not touching.

Listening.

The second heartbeat answered beneath his own.

For one impossible moment...

He thought he heard another heartbeat trying to fade away.

His instincts screamed.

"Helena."

She was already beside him.

"What?"

"He needs you."

She didn't question why.

She immediately began weaving healing magic around the boy.

His breathing steadied.

Color slowly returned to his face.

The room released a collective breath.

Helena looked at Aidan.

"You knew."

He looked at the sleeping child.

"No."

He spoke honestly.

"I only knew..."

His voice grew quieter.

"...he didn't have much time."

Outside...

Parents continued waiting beside children who still refused to wake.

Inside...

Another burden settled onto Aidan's shoulders.

He had helped save one.

Too many still remained.

And before the sun could set...

A scout rode through Stonebrook carrying another sealed message from Blackwood.

Another patrol had gone missing.

The nightmare had already moved somewhere else.

# Chapter Fifty-Two: The Village That Could Not Be Saved

The ride back to Blackwood never happened.

A second messenger found them before they reached the academy.

His horse stumbled through the mud, collapsed, and slid several feet before stopping.

The young scout rolled free.

His face was gray.

"Master Rowan..."

He could barely breathe.

"...Red Creek..."

Aidan's chest tightened.

"What happened?"

The scout swallowed hard.

"We were too late."

Silence.

No one asked another question.

They already understood.

...

Red Creek lay another hour west.

The smell reached them long before the village came into view.

Smoke.

Wet ash.

Burned timber.

Then...

Blood.

Too much.

Aidan's stomach twisted.

His body instinctively turned toward the strongest scent before he forced himself to look away.

The village gates hung open.

Homes still smoldered.

No voices.

No cries for help.

Only the crackle of dying fire.

Malik stepped over a shattered cart.

"We missed them..."

Cassian's jaw tightened.

"No."

His eyes swept the empty streets.

"They wanted us to."

Helena hurried from house to house.

Searching.

Calling.

Finding no answers.

Only the wounded who had hidden beneath collapsed floors and inside root cellars.

Too frightened to emerge.

Too late to save those who hadn't.

Aidan knelt beside an elderly man leaning against a burned wall.

The man's breathing came in shallow gasps.

He reached weakly for Aidan's sleeve.

"You came..."

His voice barely carried.

"...just..."

A cough interrupted him.

"...too late."

The words struck harder than any blow.

Aidan lowered his head.

"I'm sorry."

The old man smiled sadly.

"I know..."

His hand slipped from Aidan's sleeve.

It never moved again.

Aidan remained kneeling long after Helena quietly closed the man's eyes.

Another village.

Another apology.

Another life that had ended before he arrived.

The cost of surviving today...

Was knowing survival hadn't been enough.

...

They buried the dead before sunset.

No one spoke during the work.

The silence felt heavier than grief.

As they prepared to leave...

Aidan noticed something carved into the blackened remains of the village well.

Not words.

Only another symbol.

An eye.

Watching.

He stared at it for a long moment.

The second heartbeat answered beneath his own.

Steady.

Patient.

Waiting.

He struck the carving once with the end of his staff.

The stone cracked.

The symbol disappeared.

It changed nothing.

---

# Chapter Fifty-Three: The Breaking Point That Wasn't

Blackwood Academy received them after dark.

No sooner had the gates closed than the eastern warning runes flared crimson.

Not one.

All of them.

The alarm bell thundered across the academy.

Students ran toward shelters.

Instructors rushed for the walls.

Rowan looked toward the exhausted rescue party.

"You've barely returned."

Aidan drew a slow breath.

"I know."

His body protested the movement.

His ribs burned.

His side throbbed.

The wound in his shoulder had started bleeding again during the ride home.

He tightened the bandage with one hand and kept walking.

...

The eastern wall shook.

Not from impact.

From below.

Stone cracked beneath the defenders' feet.

Everyone spread out instinctively.

Good.

They had learned.

The ground erupted anyway.

Not beneath the largest group.

Beneath the smallest.

Three young apprentices disappeared into collapsing earth before anyone could reach them.

"Aidan!"

He was already moving.

The hole widened as loose stone continued falling inward.

He dove without thinking.

Cassian cursed.

"No!"

Too late.

Aidan vanished into the darkness.

The fall ended hard.

His injured shoulder struck first.

Pain exploded across his chest.

His vision blurred.

He forced himself upright.

The apprentices were alive.

Terrified.

One had a broken ankle.

Another bled from the forehead.

The third simply stared at him in shock.

"It's alright."

His voice remained calm.

"We're getting out."

Above...

The opening continued collapsing.

Aidan looked around.

No obvious way back.

No easy climb.

He heard Rowan shouting somewhere far overhead.

Too distant.

The apprentices looked to him.

Waiting.

Trusting.

His body had begun trembling from exhaustion.

He ignored it.

He found a narrow crack leading upward.

Too small for adults.

Large enough for children.

"You first."

They hesitated.

"What about you?"

"I'll be right behind you."

It wasn't a promise he knew he could keep.

One by one, he lifted them toward the opening.

Each movement pulled against torn muscles and fractured ribs.

By the time the last apprentice reached Cassian's outstretched hand above...

Aidan's strength failed.

The wall gave way.

Stone crashed down.

He threw one arm over his head.

Rock struck his back.

Another slammed into his side.

The tunnel collapsed around him.

Darkness swallowed everything.

For a long moment...

There was no sound.

Then...

Very faintly...

He heard voices above.

Digging.

Calling his name.

Again.

His eyes slowly closed.

He wanted to stand.

His body wouldn't answer.

His thoughts drifted.

*You came... just... too late.*

The old man's voice returned.

Then another.

The frightened child from Grey Hollow.

The instructors buried at Blackwood.

The ferryman.

The missing patrols.

The sleeping children.

Every face.

Every failure.

Every apology.

His breathing slowed.

The second heartbeat remained.

Steady.

Patient.

Waiting.

Far above...

Cassian tore at the fallen stone with his bare hands.

Malik ignored the agony in his ruined leg.

Lyra's voice broke as she called Aidan's name again and again.

Rowan never stopped giving orders.

Helena prepared the infirmary before they had even found him.

No one left.

No one surrendered.

But beneath the mountain...

For the first time in years...

Aidan allowed himself one terrifying thought.

Maybe surviving wasn't enough anymore.

# Chapter Fifty-Four: The Weight Beneath the Mountain

Darkness pressed against Aidan from every side.

Stone covered his legs.

His right arm remained pinned beneath a slab of broken rock.

Every breath sent knives through his fractured ribs.

He opened his eyes.

Nothing.

Not even a sliver of light reached him.

The silence lasted only a heartbeat.

Then...

"Aidan!"

Lyra.

Faint.

Somewhere above.

"I'm here..."

The words barely escaped his throat.

Too weak.

No one answered.

He tried to move.

The stone didn't.

Pain exploded through his shoulder.

He stopped.

Not because he wanted to.

Because his body finally refused.

Blood slowly ran beneath his coat.

Warm.

He smelled it immediately.

His breathing changed.

His stomach tightened.

No.

Not now.

He shut his eyes.

Focused on the distant voices instead.

Cassian shouting.

Malik moving debris despite his damaged leg.

Rowan calmly directing everyone.

Helena ordering healers into position.

They hadn't stopped.

They never stopped.

...

Hours passed.

Or minutes.

He couldn't tell.

Time no longer mattered beneath the mountain.

The second heartbeat continued beneath his own.

Steady.

Patient.

Waiting.

His thoughts drifted.

Not to victory.

Not to the next battle.

To the old man in Red Creek.

*You came... just... too late.*

The words refused to leave him.

Another memory followed.

Grey Hollow.

The bridge.

The children.

The buried instructors.

Every face.

Every apology.

Every promise.

He had survived all of it.

Yet someone always paid.

What good was surviving...

If he kept arriving too late?

...

Above him...

The rescue continued.

Cassian slammed another stone aside with his good arm.

The effort nearly dropped him to one knee.

Malik shoved collapsed beams away despite his permanent limp.

Blood seeped through the brace around Elias's injured leg as he reinforced unstable tunnels with trembling runes.

Lyra ignored Helena's orders to rest.

She dug until her hands bled.

No one mentioned stopping.

Rowan quietly looked at the exhausted faces around him.

"He'd still be digging for us."

No one needed more reason than that.

...

Far below...

Aidan heard it.

Not his friends.

Something else.

One slow footstep.

Then another.

Not close.

Watching.

His body tensed instinctively.

He listened.

Nothing followed.

Had he imagined it?

He couldn't be certain anymore.

That uncertainty frightened him.

He reached weakly toward his staff.

Still there.

His fingers closed around it.

If something came...

He would stand.

Even if standing killed him.

...

Stone shifted above.

A beam of light pierced the darkness.

"Aidan!"

This time he heard Lyra clearly.

He managed the faintest answer.

"I'm..."

He coughed blood.

"...here."

The digging became frantic.

Hope surged through the tunnel.

Another stone moved.

Another.

Then—

The entire ceiling groaned.

Rowan's expression changed instantly.

"Back!"

Too late.

The weakened chamber collapsed again.

Fresh rock thundered into the opening.

Lyra disappeared beneath a cloud of dust.

Cassian barely pulled her backward before another section gave way.

The tunnel sealed itself again.

Silence returned.

Below...

Aidan stared at the place where the light had vanished.

They had been so close.

His hand slowly fell away from the opening.

Hope had reached him.

Then the mountain had taken it back.

---

# Chapter Fifty-Five: Another Scar

Blackwood Academy worked through the entire night.

No one slept.

Fresh rescue teams replaced exhausted ones every hour.

Even first-year students carried broken stone away from the collapse.

The academy itself seemed determined not to lose another person.

...

Professor Helena stood beside Rowan.

"He won't survive another collapse."

Rowan never looked away from the mountain.

"Then we won't give it one."

"He has internal bleeding."

"I know."

"He hasn't eaten properly in weeks."

"I know."

"He's exhausted."

"I know."

She lowered her voice.

"We're running out of time."

For the first time...

The old master's shoulders seemed heavier.

"So is he."

...

Below...

Aidan's breathing had become slower.

Not calmer.

Weaker.

His side no longer hurt the way it had.

That frightened him.

Pain meant he was still fighting.

The absence of it felt wrong.

He forced himself awake.

He refused to drift.

His hearing reached upward again.

Stone.

Voices.

Tools striking rock.

Then...

Something else.

A faint scratching.

Not above.

Behind him.

His body reacted before his thoughts.

He turned as much as the stone allowed.

Darkness.

Nothing visible.

The scratching stopped.

He waited.

Minutes passed.

No sound returned.

Had it been loose rock?

His imagination?

Or something watching from another tunnel?

He couldn't know.

The not knowing settled into him like another wound.

...

At last...

A narrow crack opened above his shoulder.

A hand reached through.

Cassian's.

"I've got you."

His voice was rough.

Exhausted.

Real.

Aidan reached back.

Their hands locked together.

Stone shifted.

More hands reached through.

Malik.

Rowan.

Together they widened the opening enough to pull him free.

The moment Aidan reached the surface...

His legs gave out.

He didn't even try to stand.

Helena was already beside him.

She pressed both hands against his side.

Blood soaked through immediately.

"We're losing him!"

Healers rushed forward.

Lyra dropped to her knees beside him.

Tears streaked through the dust covering her face.

"You idiot..."

Her voice shook.

"You told us you'd be right behind them."

Aidan looked at her.

A faint smile almost appeared.

"I'm..."

His voice barely carried.

"...sorry."

She shook her head fiercely.

"No."

She gripped his hand.

"No more apologizing."

His eyes slowly closed.

Not from peace.

From exhaustion.

As Helena fought to keep him conscious...

Aidan heard the academy around him.

Boots.

Voices.

Prayers.

Someone crying.

Someone thanking him for saving the apprentices.

He felt none of it.

Only emptiness.

He had survived again.

The cost...

Was another piece of himself he could no longer find.

Far beyond Blackwood's walls...

Rain began falling over the northern forests.

Whatever waited there remained unseen.

Unanswered.

And while Blackwood struggled simply to keep its Crimson Witch alive...

Another burden was already beginning somewhere beyond the rain.

# Chapter Fifty-Six: The Price of Standing

Aidan woke to darkness.

Not underground.

The infirmary.

Rain tapped softly against the windows.

The familiar scent of herbs lingered in the air.

Every part of his body hurt.

For one brief moment...

He almost welcomed it.

Pain meant he was still alive.

The thought no longer comforted him.

...

"You should still be unconscious."

Helena's voice broke the silence.

She stood beside his bed, exhaustion etched into every line of her face.

"You've been asleep for almost two days."

Aidan tried to sit.

The room spun violently.

Pain tore through his ribs.

His side burned beneath layers of fresh bandages.

His shoulder refused to lift higher than his chest.

Helena gently pushed him back.

"No."

"I have patrol."

"No."

"The rescue reports."

"No."

"The northern villages—"

"No."

Every answer came firmer than the last.

"You almost died."

Silence.

Finally Aidan asked the question that truly mattered.

"The apprentices?"

"They're alive."

His shoulders relaxed.

Only then.

Helena noticed.

He had asked about everyone else before himself.

Again.

...

Later that afternoon...

Rowan entered alone.

Neither spoke immediately.

The old master quietly placed another weathered map beside the bed.

Three fresh marks.

Three separate villages.

"These came while you were unconscious."

Aidan closed his eyes.

"How bad?"

"One caravan disappeared."

He moved one marker.

"A bridge collapsed."

Another.

"And an entire watch patrol never returned."

The last marker remained untouched.

"We haven't found them."

Aidan slowly sat upright despite Helena's orders.

Every movement hurt.

He ignored it.

"I'll go."

"No."

Rowan's answer came without hesitation.

"You can barely breathe."

"They still need someone."

"They need you alive."

Aidan looked toward the rain outside.

"If I stay here..."

He swallowed.

"...someone else won't."

The room fell silent.

Neither man won.

Neither man was wrong.

...

By evening...

Aidan had left the infirmary anyway.

His pace was slower.

His breathing shallower.

The academy noticed immediately.

Students stopped pretending not to stare.

Some smiled with relief.

Others looked frightened.

Not because he was injured.

Because they had begun believing he couldn't be.

He walked past them quietly.

One first-year rushed forward.

"Master Aidan..."

He still wasn't used to hearing that title.

"We repaired the eastern barrier."

The young witch smiled proudly.

"We did it the way you taught us."

For the first time in days...

Aidan almost smiled.

"Good."

Only one word.

Yet the student beamed as though he'd received the greatest praise imaginable.

Aidan continued walking.

The moment passed.

His smile disappeared before anyone else noticed it.

---

# Chapter Fifty-Seven: What the Rain Revealed

The rain finally stopped.

Fog replaced it.

Heavy enough that Blackwood's outer walls vanished into white.

Perfect weather for another disappearance.

Everyone knew it.

Patrols doubled.

Again.

Messengers traveled in groups of four instead of two.

Again.

The academy adapted.

Again.

Yet the uneasiness remained.

...

Aidan insisted on joining the northern patrol.

Helena argued until her voice grew hoarse.

Rowan remained silent.

When Aidan finally stood before the gates fully dressed for travel...

The old master simply handed him a new walking staff.

"Lean on this."

It wasn't permission.

It wasn't refusal.

It was acceptance that Aidan would go regardless.

...

The forest felt different.

Not hostile.

Empty.

Even Malik noticed.

"I hate this."

Cassian nodded.

"I'd rather hear something."

The silence pressed against them.

Every snapped twig sounded too loud.

Every drifting branch became another question.

Halfway through the march...

Aidan stopped so suddenly Lyra nearly walked into him.

"What?"

He didn't answer.

His hearing stretched through the fog.

Water.

Wind.

Then...

Footsteps.

Running.

Not toward them.

Away.

Fast.

"Someone's moving."

"Where?"

He pointed.

Everyone ran.

The chase lasted only minutes.

It ended beside a narrow ravine.

Fresh footprints stopped at the edge.

No sign of climbing down.

No sign of jumping across.

Simply...

Gone.

Cassian studied the mud.

"Impossible."

"No."

Aidan knelt despite the pain pulling through his side.

His fingers brushed one of the prints.

Still warm.

Whoever had been here had vanished only moments before.

A faint sound drifted upward from the ravine.

Not a voice.

Not an animal.

Rock.

Breaking.

Very deep below.

His instincts screamed.

"Back."

No one questioned him.

The patrol retreated.

Three heartbeats later...

The entire edge of the ravine collapsed into the darkness.

Earth thundered downward.

Trees disappeared.

The place where they had stood seconds earlier simply ceased to exist.

Malik stared into the widening chasm.

"If we'd stayed..."

"We'd be buried."

Aidan finished quietly.

His knees buckled.

The sudden movement reopened the wound along his side.

Blood soaked through the fresh bandages almost immediately.

Lyra caught him before he hit the ground.

"You shouldn't even be here."

He leaned heavily against the new staff Rowan had given him.

"I know."

His voice sounded distant.

Almost detached.

"I just..."

He looked toward the shattered ravine.

"...couldn't stay behind."

No one answered.

Because every one of them understood.

As they turned back toward Blackwood...

Aidan glanced once more into the fog.

For the briefest instant...

He thought he saw a lone figure standing across the ravine.

Still.

Watching.

The fog shifted.

Nothing remained.

He said nothing.

Not because he doubted what he had seen.

Because he no longer trusted the difference between what the forest showed him...

And what it wanted him to see.

Behind them, unnoticed by the patrol, a fresh rockslide sealed the ravine completely.

Whatever answers might have existed below...

Were buried before anyone had the chance to ask the questions.

# Chapter Fifty-Eight: The Weight of Returning

The walk back to Blackwood was slower than the journey out.

Not because the path had changed.

Because Aidan had.

Every few hundred steps he leaned a little harder on Rowan's staff.

He tried to hide it.

No one pretended not to notice.

Malik quietly shortened the pace.

Cassian walked close enough to catch him if he stumbled.

Lyra never drifted more than a few feet away.

No one mentioned why.

...

The academy gates appeared through the fading fog.

Before anyone could breathe with relief—

The eastern warning rune flashed once.

Then the western.

Then the northern.

Not an alarm.

Three separate reports arriving at once.

Aidan closed his eyes.

"Again..."

A messenger reached Rowan first.

"The northern bridge held."

A second interrupted him.

"The western grain convoy is overdue."

A third stumbled into the courtyard.

"The eastern watchfire never lit."

Three problems.

None could wait.

Elias spread another map across the nearest table.

"If we divide again—"

"They'll expect it," Cassian finished.

"If we don't divide," Malik said quietly, "someone goes without help."

There was no right answer.

Only different losses.

...

Aidan's hearing drifted across the courtyard.

Students.

Rainwater dripping from the roofs.

A frightened horse.

Then—

Someone crying.

A little girl.

Near the infirmary.

He turned before realizing he had moved.

A young apprentice sat beside her injured brother.

The boy's arm hung awkwardly inside a splint.

"I'm sorry," she whispered to him.

"I shouldn't have asked you to climb the wall."

The boy forced a smile.

"It isn't your fault."

Aidan stood watching for a moment.

Neither child noticed him.

His chest tightened.

Not from broken ribs.

From memory.

Every apology sounded the same now.

...

He continued toward the strategy hall.

Helena intercepted him halfway.

"You're bleeding again."

He looked down.

The bandages around his side had turned dark.

"I didn't feel it."

"I know."

She guided him toward the infirmary.

He resisted for exactly three steps.

Then stopped.

Not because he agreed.

Because he suddenly felt tired enough to fall where he stood.

...

Helena cut away the soaked bandages.

The room fell silent.

The wound hadn't merely reopened.

The stitches had torn completely.

She looked at him for a long time before speaking.

"You've stopped noticing injuries."

"I notice."

"No."

She gently cleaned dried blood from his side.

"You notice when other people are hurt."

Her voice became quieter.

"Your own body has become background noise."

He had no answer.

Because she was right.

...

That evening Rowan made a decision no one expected.

"Aidan stays inside the academy tomorrow."

The room immediately objected.

"Aidan—"

"I'll go," he interrupted.

"No."

Rowan's voice carried the same calm certainty it always had.

"This is not punishment."

"It feels like it."

"It is necessity."

Silence.

"You cannot protect anyone if you die walking to the gate."

Aidan looked away.

His hands slowly tightened.

He understood.

He hated it.

Because somewhere...

Someone needed help.

And he wouldn't be there.

---

# Chapter Fifty-Nine: The Burden of Staying

Morning arrived without Aidan leaving Blackwood.

It felt wrong.

Every instinct urged him toward the gates.

Toward another patrol.

Another village.

Another burden.

Instead...

He stood inside the eastern training grounds helping reinforce defensive barriers with the apprentices.

His movements were slower than before.

His breathing shallower.

Yet his instructions remained steady.

"Again."

The students repeated the formation.

"No."

He quietly adjusted one apprentice's stance.

"Anchor with your whole body."

She tried again.

The barrier held.

Better.

A faint smile crossed her face.

"You remembered."

"You taught us."

The words lingered.

He wondered how many lessons he had forgotten teaching.

...

By midday...

The first rescue team returned.

Not everyone came back.

A wagon rolled through the gates carrying two wounded guards.

Another arrived carrying one covered body.

The courtyard became silent.

Aidan's hands stopped moving.

He had obeyed Rowan.

He had stayed.

Someone had died anyway.

The thought settled heavily inside him.

Would he have changed the outcome?

He didn't know.

The uncertainty hurt more than certainty ever could.

...

Cassian found him alone beneath the old oak.

"You heard the wagons."

Aidan nodded.

"I counted them."

Cassian wasn't surprised.

"You couldn't have been everywhere."

"I know."

"You keep saying that."

"Because it's true."

Cassian looked toward the fresh grave already being prepared.

"And you still blame yourself."

Neither denied it.

Neither could.

...

As evening settled over Blackwood...

Aidan remained beneath the branches of the old oak.

The academy slowly quieted.

His hearing stretched beyond the walls.

Toward distant villages.

Toward empty forests.

Toward places he couldn't reach.

Then...

One heartbeat.

Very faint.

Far away.

It lasted only an instant before disappearing.

He opened his eyes.

The second heartbeat answered beneath his own.

Steady.

Patient.

Waiting.

He didn't follow.

He couldn't.

Not tonight.

For the first time in years...

The burden wasn't another battle.

It was remaining where he was while not knowing what might be happening somewhere else.

And somehow...

That hurt even more than the wounds still stitched across his body.

# Chapter Sixty: The Price of Remaining

The grave had not been filled before the next alarm sounded.

One long bell.

Then another.

Not from Blackwood.

From the southern ridge.

A signal fire.

Rowan looked toward the smoke.

"No."

His voice was almost weary.

"Already..."

No one asked if they were leaving.

Everyone simply moved.

Aidan reached for his coat.

Helena caught his wrist.

"You are not fit to fight."

"I know."

"You've reopened every major wound."

"I know."

"You can barely stand."

"I know."

He gently pulled free.

"They still lit the signal."

There was nothing Helena could say that outweighed those words.

...

The southern ridge had become a battlefield before Blackwood arrived.

Wagons lay overturned.

Trees had been split apart.

The survivors huddled beneath shattered carts while instructors from a nearby outpost fought desperately to keep them alive.

Aidan entered the fight already exhausted.

His first barrier held.

The second nearly failed.

By the third...

His vision blurred.

Something slammed into him from the side.

He never saw it coming.

His body twisted on instinct, sparing his chest.

The blow still hurled him through a stone wall.

The impact shattered what little healing his ribs had managed.

He hit the ground hard enough that breathing became impossible.

For several terrifying seconds...

He couldn't move.

Someone screamed his name.

Another impact.

Another collapse.

Cassian appeared over him, intercepting another attack before it reached Aidan.

His damaged arm folded beneath the force.

He stayed on his feet anyway.

"Move!"

Aidan forced air back into his lungs.

Pain flooded every nerve.

He stood.

Not because he had recovered.

Because other people still needed him standing.

The battle continued.

When it finally ended...

Three more defenders would never return to Blackwood.

The caravan survived.

The people survived.

The cost remained behind on the hillside.

...

As the wounded were loaded onto wagons, Malik quietly sat beside Aidan.

Neither spoke.

After a long silence, Malik finally asked,

"Do you remember when we used to think passing Rowan's exams was hard?"

Aidan looked toward the stretchers.

"I don't remember much before this."

Malik didn't laugh.

Neither of them could.

# Chapter Sixty-One: The Weight That Never Lifted

Blackwood Academy no longer measured time by weeks.

It measured it by casualties.

Another name joined the memorial wall.

Students walked past it in silence.

No one had room for fresh grief before another responsibility arrived.

...

Helena removed Aidan's blood-soaked bandages once again.

This time...

She stopped halfway through.

The room remained quiet.

Aidan looked up.

"What?"

She traced the scar tissue surrounding older wounds.

"There isn't enough healthy tissue left."

He frowned.

"What do you mean?"

"Every wound keeps reopening before the last one finishes healing."

She looked into his tired eyes.

"Your body is surviving."

Her voice broke almost imperceptibly.

"But it has stopped remembering what uninjured feels like."

The words lingered.

Neither of them spoke again.

...

That afternoon, Aidan finally sat down to study the strange charcoal map recovered days earlier.

He had barely unfolded it—

The western alarm bell rang.

Once.

Twice.

Then continuously.

Aidan closed the map without reading another line.

Another crisis.

Another choice already made.

He was halfway across the courtyard before anyone called after him.

"Aidan!"

He never slowed.

His side tore open before he reached the gate.

Warm blood spread beneath fresh bandages.

He didn't stop.

He didn't even look down.

By the time Rowan caught up beside him, Aidan's breathing had already become uneven.

"You should still be in the infirmary."

"So should half this academy."

Neither man said another word.

They ran together toward another emergency.

Behind them...

The unfinished map remained on the strategy table.

Its unanswered questions would have to wait.

Again.

Because every time Aidan reached toward the truth...

Someone else needed saving first.

And with every life he chose over himself...

Another piece of the Crimson Witch was left behind on the road.

# Chapter Sixty-Two: The Battle He Couldn't Win

The western alarm bell was still ringing when Aidan reached Blackwood's outer gate.

His side had already soaked through another set of bandages.

He ignored it.

The road west was crowded with terrified villagers.

Children cried.

Parents carried whatever they could save.

Nobody looked back.

"Aidan!"

An old woman grabbed his sleeve as he passed.

"They're still trapped!"

"Where?"

She pointed toward the hills.

"Our people never made it out."

He looked toward Rowan.

The old master had already begun directing evacuations.

"Get everyone inside the walls."

Cassian and Malik immediately took charge.

Lyra helped the elderly and wounded.

Helena transformed the entrance hall into another emergency infirmary.

Everyone had a task.

Aidan had one too.

The hills.

...

The climb became steeper with every step.

His breathing grew uneven.

His shoulder throbbed.

Every stride sent fresh pain through fractured ribs.

He kept climbing.

Halfway up...

He stopped.

The forest was wrong.

No wind.

No birds.

No insects.

Only silence.

Then...

A scream.

A child.

Without thinking...

He ran.

Branches tore across his face.

Loose stones rolled beneath his boots.

He burst into a narrow ravine.

Empty.

No child.

No footprints.

Nothing.

His instincts screamed.

Too late.

The hillside exploded.

Stone and fallen trees crashed down from above.

Aidan threw both hands upward.

"Hold!"

Crimson light slowed the avalanche.

Not stopped.

Slowed.

The weight was too great.

The mountain itself pushed against him.

Blood streamed from his nose.

His vision blurred.

One boulder struck his left shoulder.

Another slammed into his ribs.

His barrier shattered.

The remaining stones buried him beneath a wave of earth.

...

Cassian heard the collapse from below.

"Aidan!"

Without hesitation he sprinted toward the ravine.

Rowan grabbed his arm.

"No!"

"He's alive."

"How do you know?"

Cassian looked toward the mountain.

"Because he'd still be fighting."

No one argued.

They climbed.

...

Beneath the rubble...

Aidan forced one arm free.

Every breath felt smaller than the last.

His hearing barely reached beyond the stone surrounding him.

Then...

Voices.

Not close.

His friends.

Digging again.

Always digging.

He almost laughed.

Instead...

He coughed blood.

His hand found his staff.

He drove its end into the packed earth above him.

Once.

Twice.

A third time.

A faint knock echoed upward.

Cassian stopped instantly.

"There!"

Every shovel turned.

Every spell shifted.

Every ounce of strength focused toward that single sound.

...

When they finally pulled him free...

He couldn't stand.

His left shoulder hung unnaturally low.

Helena looked once before speaking.

"It's dislocated."

She reset it immediately.

The crack echoed through the ravine.

Aidan didn't cry out.

He simply closed his eyes until the pain settled enough to breathe again.

"You need rest," Helena whispered.

He looked past her toward the frightened villagers.

"They need homes."

Helena lowered her head.

She no longer knew how to argue with someone who had forgotten how to choose himself.

---

# Chapter Sixty-Three: The Cost of Another Tomorrow

Blackwood's walls filled beyond capacity.

The refugees from the western hills slept wherever space remained.

Classrooms.

Hallways.

Training yards.

Even the Great Hall became a shelter.

Students gave up their beds without complaint.

No one believed the displacement would last only a few days.

...

Aidan spent the night carrying supplies.

Not because he had recovered.

Because there weren't enough hands.

His shoulder remained wrapped tightly against his chest.

His side bled whenever he lifted anything heavier than a sack of grain.

He carried it anyway.

Malik finally stepped in front of him.

"Enough."

"I'm almost done."

"You've said that since yesterday."

"There are still people waiting."

"There always will be."

The words struck harder than Malik intended.

Because they were true.

There would always be another person waiting.

Another village.

Another burden.

Another reason to keep walking.

...

Near dawn...

Elias entered the strategy hall carrying a bundle of weathered patrol journals recovered from an abandoned outpost.

"I thought these might help."

Aidan reached toward the first journal.

Before he could open it—

The southern warning rune ignited.

Then the eastern.

Then the western.

Three directions.

Three separate emergencies.

Again.

Aidan slowly withdrew his hand from the unopened journal.

He didn't even sigh anymore.

He simply stood.

Cassian watched him quietly.

"You were trying to find answers."

"I know."

"You never get the chance."

"I know."

The journal remained unopened on the table.

Another truth postponed.

Another mystery left waiting.

Because somewhere outside Blackwood...

Someone needed saving first.

...

As the rescue parties assembled...

Lyra stepped beside Aidan.

She looked carefully at the fresh blood spreading through the bandages around his side.

"You haven't healed from the mountain."

"No."

"You haven't healed from Red Creek."

"No."

"You haven't healed from the reservoir."

"No."

She swallowed.

"You haven't healed in years."

For the first time...

Aidan didn't answer immediately.

His tired eyes drifted toward the academy gates already opening for another mission.

Finally...

He spoke.

"I don't think I've had time."

Lyra looked away before he could see the tears forming in her eyes.

She remembered the boy who had laughed through Rowan's impossible lessons.

Who complained about long training sessions.

Who argued with Malik over the last piece of bread.

Who smiled without effort.

Standing beside her now...

He was still Aidan.

Still kind.

Still willing to give everything for someone else.

But every rescue took something that never came back.

The gates opened fully.

Another messenger stumbled through.

Another desperate plea.

Another impossible choice.

Aidan adjusted the sling supporting his injured shoulder.

Tightened the bandages around his ribs.

Ignored the blood staining his side.

Then walked toward the next battle before the last one had even finished costing him.

# Chapter Sixty-Four: The Ones He Couldn't Reach

The messenger never reached the strategy hall.

He collapsed inside Blackwood's gates.

"North..."

His voice cracked.

"...please..."

Then nothing.

Helena rushed forward.

She found a pulse.

Weak.

Barely.

Aidan was already moving.

No discussion.

No planning.

Only action.

...

The northern valley lay under a curtain of smoke.

Not fresh.

Hours old.

Too late.

The realization settled over the rescue party long before they reached the village.

They found survivors.

Not many.

A little girl sat beside a burned doorway holding the hand of a woman who would never wake again.

She looked up as Aidan approached.

"You came."

Her voice held no anger.

Only exhaustion.

He knelt despite the pain tearing through his ribs.

"I'm sorry."

She nodded once.

"I know."

She had heard those words before.

Perhaps from someone else.

Perhaps only in stories.

Now they belonged to him too.

...

The search continued.

Every house held another answer they wished they hadn't found.

A father beneath collapsed beams.

A healer beside an empty medicine chest.

A patrol of six.

Only two still breathed.

Cassian helped lift broken timber.

Malik carried the wounded toward the wagons despite the agony in his damaged leg.

Lyra wrapped frightened children in blankets.

Elias quietly wrote names.

Someone had to remember them.

Aidan moved from one ruin to the next.

His shoulder burned with every lift.

His side bled through fresh bandages.

His breathing remained uneven.

He stopped beside one destroyed home.

Too late.

Again.

He closed his eyes.

One slow breath.

Then another.

"Move."

Only one word.

The rescue continued.

...

They left the valley carrying fewer people than they had hoped.

More than they had feared.

It did not feel like victory.

No one called it one.

# Chapter Sixty-Five: The Law That Failed

The wagons had barely crossed Blackwood's gates when the western alarm bell began screaming.

Not ringing.

Screaming.

Three guards stood atop the walls waving crimson signal flags.

The western bridge.

Again.

Aidan looked toward Rowan.

The old master saw the exhaustion written across every face.

"We don't have time."

No one did.

...

The bridge had already begun collapsing before they arrived.

Nearly forty civilians remained trapped on the far side.

Rain had swollen the river below into a violent torrent.

The old supports groaned beneath impossible strain.

Aidan stepped onto the bridge.

"Stay behind me."

His voice was quiet.

Steady.

Cassian moved beside him.

"No."

Aidan didn't look back.

"I'll hold them."

He raised both hands.

"Hold."

Crimson light spread across the fractured bridge.

The timbers stopped falling.

For one heartbeat.

Then another.

People ran.

Children first.

Then the elderly.

Then the wounded.

The bridge shook violently.

Aidan's breathing became ragged.

Blood ran freely from his nose.

His injured shoulder trembled uncontrollably.

The Law flickered.

"Aidan!" Lyra shouted.

He didn't answer.

The bridge lurched again.

His vision blurred.

He forced more will into the Law.

The crimson light brightened.

Then—

It shattered.

The Law broke.

The bridge collapsed.

Cassian leaped forward, catching one child before the river claimed her.

Malik threw himself toward an elderly man, dragging him onto solid ground.

Lyra caught another survivor by the wrist.

Not everyone made it.

The river swallowed several figures before anyone could reach them.

Aidan fell to one knee.

His chest heaved.

He stared at the water rushing beneath the broken bridge.

For the first time since becoming the Crimson Witch...

His Law had failed because his body could no longer sustain it.

No lack of knowledge.

No lack of will.

Only exhaustion.

Helena reached him moments later.

He didn't look at her.

He kept watching the river.

"They were..."

His voice nearly disappeared.

"...too slow."

Helena followed his gaze.

"No."

Her answer came softly.

"You were asked to hold up the impossible."

He never replied.

The survivors gathered on the riverbank.

Some embraced.

Some wept.

Some searched desperately downstream for people who would never return.

Behind them...

The broken bridge drifted away piece by piece.

Aidan remained kneeling.

Water roared below.

His breathing wouldn't steady.

His hands wouldn't stop shaking.

He had saved many.

He had not saved all.

This time...

He had watched the moment hope slipped through his own fingers.

And when Rowan quietly placed a hand on his shoulder, Aidan did not speak.

He simply stood.

Turned toward the next road.

And began walking.

Because somewhere else...

Another bell would ring.

And someone would still be waiting.

# Chapter Sixty-Six: Fractures

The road from the broken bridge felt longer than it had that morning.

No one spoke.

The wagons rolled behind them carrying the wounded.

Empty blankets covered the places where others should have been.

The rain had washed the blood from the road.

Not from memory.

...

Blackwood Academy opened its gates before the column arrived.

Students rushed forward with stretchers.

Healers divided the injured into rows.

Professor Helena moved from patient to patient without pausing.

She reached Aidan last.

He was still standing.

Barely.

His right hand trembled so badly he could no longer hide it by gripping his staff.

"Sit."

He didn't answer.

"Aidan."

"I can still—"

His knees buckled.

Cassian caught him before he struck the ground.

For a heartbeat neither man moved.

"I've got you," Cassian said quietly.

Aidan nodded once.

Nothing more.

...

Helena unwrapped the bandages in silence.

The bruising across his ribs had darkened nearly black.

Fresh blood seeped from the wound along his side.

His shoulder had swollen again after holding the bridge.

She rested her hands on the edge of the bed.

"You reached your limit."

Aidan stared at the ceiling.

"It wasn't enough."

"No."

Her voice carried no judgment.

"It wasn't."

The honesty hurt more than comfort would have.

...

That evening Rowan gathered only the inner circle.

No students.

No instructors.

Just the people who had carried the weight with Aidan for years.

"The attacks are accelerating," Elias said, spreading the newest reports across the table.

"They're forcing us to choose between places again," Malik added.

Cassian remained quiet.

He watched Aidan instead.

Aidan hadn't looked up once.

His eyes remained fixed on the map.

Not studying it.

Simply staring.

Finally Rowan spoke.

"We cannot answer every call."

Silence.

No one argued.

Because everyone knew he was right.

Aidan closed his eyes.

For years he had believed effort could overcome impossibility.

Now...

Even effort had limits.

...

Later that night he returned to the old oak.

The academy behind him remained awake.

The infirmary lights never went out anymore.

His breathing remained uneven.

Every deep breath reminded him of the bridge.

Every shallow one reminded him of the people he couldn't save.

The second heartbeat echoed beneath his own.

Steady.

Patient.

Waiting.

He rested one hand against the bark.

"I tried."

The wind carried the words away.

No answer came.

Only silence.

---

# Chapter Sixty-Seven: The Narrowing Road

Morning began with another report.

Not an attack.

A withdrawal.

One of Blackwood's outlying watch posts had been abandoned during the night.

The defenders had fallen back rather than be surrounded.

It was the first time Rowan had ordered ground given up willingly.

The academy no longer had enough people to hold every position.

That truth settled heavily over everyone.

...

Aidan volunteered for the relief column.

Rowan looked at him for a long moment.

"You can barely lift your left arm."

"I can still walk."

"You couldn't keep the bridge standing."

Aidan lowered his eyes.

"I know."

The words remained between them.

Not accusation.

Fact.

Rowan finally nodded.

"Then walk."

...

The abandoned watch post overlooked a narrow mountain pass.

Barricades had been left behind.

Supply crates destroyed so they could not be used by whoever came after.

Everything had been orderly.

Intentional.

No panic.

Only retreat.

Cassian stood beside Aidan overlooking the valley.

"We're giving up ground."

"We're keeping people."

Cassian nodded.

"It still feels like losing."

"It is."

The answer came quietly.

Neither tried to soften it.

...

As the column prepared to return, Aidan noticed something on the weathered stone beside the old signal tower.

Not a new symbol.

Not a clue.

Just fresh scratches where someone had deliberately erased something that had once been there.

He looked at it for a long moment.

Then turned away.

There were no answers to gather.

Only another road to walk.

Another group to protect.

Another day to survive.

Behind him, Cassian watched in silence.

For the first time, he saw not only Aidan's wounds...

But the way he carried them.

Not with defiance.

Not with hope.

Simply because there were still people behind him who needed someone to keep walking.

And that realization frightened Cassian more than the battles ever had.

# Chapter Sixty-Eight: What Remained

Blackwood Academy no longer waited for dawn.

The wounded arrived before it.

The dead arrived after.

The pattern had become so familiar that no one looked surprised when another wagon rolled through the gates beneath a sky still filled with stars.

Helena walked toward it.

She already knew what she would find.

Bandages.

Blood.

Silence.

...

Aidan stood nearby helping unload the survivors.

His left arm remained secured against his side beneath a leather brace.

Every movement tugged against the torn muscles across his ribs.

He never reached with that arm anymore.

His body had quietly learned its limits.

His heart had not.

A frightened boy stumbled while climbing from the wagon.

Before anyone else moved...

Aidan caught him with his good arm.

"Easy."

Only one word.

The boy nodded.

"Thank you."

Aidan simply guided him toward the infirmary.

He never mentioned the fresh blood now soaking through his own bandages.

...

That afternoon Rowan assembled the remaining patrol captains.

The map had changed again.

Three roads were crossed out.

Two villages had been evacuated permanently.

One watchtower would not be rebuilt.

"We're shrinking our line," Rowan said.

No one objected.

Every argument had already been lost weeks earlier.

Aidan studied the map.

Not searching for another solution.

Accepting the one before him.

"We can't hold everything."

His voice was calm.

Quiet.

Matter-of-fact.

The room fell silent.

Cassian looked at him.

Months ago...

Aidan would have searched for another way.

Now...

He simply measured what could still be saved.

...

The meeting ended.

Everyone left.

Cassian remained behind.

"Aidan."

He stopped.

"When did you stop arguing with Rowan?"

Aidan considered the question.

"I don't know."

"You always challenged him."

"I was wrong."

"No."

Cassian stepped closer.

"You believed impossible things."

Aidan looked toward the academy courtyard.

Children carried water between buildings.

Students repaired shattered stone.

Life continued because someone chose to keep it moving.

"I still do."

Cassian frowned.

"Then why don't you fight anymore?"

Aidan answered without turning.

"I do."

His hand rested lightly against the map.

"I just stopped fighting reality."

The words stayed with Cassian long after Aidan walked away.

...

That night...

Lyra found Aidan beneath the old oak.

He wasn't watching the forest.

He wasn't listening.

He simply sat with his back against the ancient trunk.

Eyes closed.

Breathing slowly.

"You fell asleep here."

He opened one eye.

"For a minute."

"It was almost an hour."

"I didn't mean to."

She sat beside him.

Neither spoke.

The silence wasn't uncomfortable.

It was simply all either of them had left.

Finally Lyra leaned her head gently against his shoulder.

"You don't have to carry everything."

He didn't answer immediately.

When he finally spoke...

His voice was almost too quiet to hear.

"I know."

She waited.

"But if I put it down..."

He looked toward the lights of Blackwood.

"...someone else picks it up."

He closed his eyes again.

"I'd rather it stay with me."

Lyra reached for his hand.

He squeezed hers once.

Then they sat beneath the old oak until dawn found them both still awake.

---

# Chapter Sixty-Nine: The Last Good Choice

The call came before sunrise.

Not from a messenger.

From the academy wards themselves.

Three outer barriers failed within the same minute.

Not destroyed.

Abandoned.

The patrol assigned to them had fallen back.

Rowan read the report only once.

"They're driving people toward East Hollow."

Elias spread another map across the table.

"If the refugees reach the gorge before we do..."

"They'll be trapped," Malik finished.

No one wasted another word.

They moved.

...

East Hollow lay between steep cliffs and a fast-moving river.

By the time Blackwood arrived...

Hundreds of civilians crowded the narrow road.

Parents carried infants.

Older children pulled younger siblings by the hand.

Some looked back constantly.

Others no longer looked back at all.

Panic spread faster than orders.

Aidan stepped into the center of the road.

His voice barely rose above the noise.

"Move."

People listened.

Not because he shouted.

Because they recognized him.

The line began moving again.

Slowly.

Order replaced panic.

For a little while.

...

A crack echoed through the gorge.

The cliff above them shifted.

Loose stone rained onto the road.

Not enough to crush everyone.

Enough to block the only escape.

The front of the column stopped.

The rear kept pushing.

People stumbled.

Children cried.

The pressure threatened to become more dangerous than the falling rock.

Aidan climbed onto the broken stone.

His injured shoulder nearly gave out pulling himself upward.

He ignored it.

Both hands rose.

"Hold."

Crimson light spread across the fractured cliff.

It answered.

Weakly.

The stone slowed.

Not stopped.

Only slowed.

His Law had become thinner.

Strained.

His breathing grew ragged.

Blood ran from his nose again.

His knees trembled.

Cassian reached the base of the slope.

"You can't keep this up."

"I know."

The words came through clenched teeth.

"Keep them moving."

Cassian looked toward the civilians.

Then back at Aidan.

For one long moment...

Neither man spoke.

Then Cassian nodded once.

He turned away immediately.

"Move!"

His voice thundered through the gorge.

"Don't stop!"

Families ran.

Children crossed.

The elderly were carried.

Lyra and Malik pulled people through the narrowing gap.

Elias reinforced the path as best he could.

One by one...

The road emptied.

Aidan remained where he was.

Every heartbeat cost more than the last.

His vision darkened around the edges.

The Law flickered.

He steadied it.

Not because he believed he would walk away unharmed.

Because there were still people behind him.

The last little boy reached safety.

He looked back.

Their eyes met.

Aidan managed the smallest nod.

Go.

The boy ran.

Only then...

Only when he knew every civilian had crossed...

Did Aidan let the Law go.

The mountainside came down with a roar.

Stone swallowed the road behind the fleeing survivors.

Dust climbed into the morning sky.

When it settled...

The escape route was gone.

The people had lived.

The passage had not.

Cassian stood on the far side of the collapse, staring through the drifting dust toward where Aidan had been only moments before.

He could no longer see him.

Only broken stone.

And the terrible certainty that every time Aidan chose another life over his own...

The distance between the man he had always known...

And the fate waiting for him...

Grew smaller.

# Chapter Seventy: Beneath the Fallen Mountain

For one terrible heartbeat...

No one moved.

Dust rolled through the gorge, swallowing everything beyond the collapsed mountainside.

Then Lyra screamed.

"Aidan!"

She lunged toward the wall of broken stone.

Cassian caught her before loose rock buried them both.

"It's still falling!"

"Let me go!"

She fought against him with every ounce of strength she had left.

"He'll be trapped!"

"I know!"

His voice cracked.

"I know."

...

Rowan reached the collapse seconds later.

His eyes swept across the shattered cliff.

No opening.

No path through.

Only thousands of tons of broken mountain.

"Dig."

The order came without hesitation.

Nobody questioned whether it was possible.

Malik drove his shovel into the rubble.

Students who had escaped the gorge returned without being asked.

Parents handed frightened children to strangers and joined the rescue.

The people Aidan had saved refused to leave him behind.

...

Far beneath the collapse...

Aidan slowly opened one eye.

Darkness.

Again.

His first thought wasn't fear.

It was disappointment.

*Not again.*

He tried to breathe.

Something heavy pressed across his legs.

His left shoulder screamed.

His ribs felt as though they had finally shattered completely.

He closed his eyes.

The second heartbeat continued beneath his own.

Steady.

Patient.

Waiting.

He listened.

Nothing.

Not yet.

Then...

Very faintly...

Stone.

Moving.

They were digging.

Of course they were.

He leaned his head against cold rock.

"They never quit..."

The words barely escaped his lips.

...

Hours passed.

He drifted in and out of consciousness.

Sometimes he heard Lyra shouting.

Sometimes Cassian.

Sometimes only the mountain settling around him.

More than once he reached for his staff.

His fingers no longer had the strength to close around it.

Blood had soaked through every remaining bandage.

His breathing became shallower.

Not because he wanted to stop.

Because his body had less and less left to give.

...

Above...

Helena grabbed Rowan's arm.

"He can't survive another full collapse."

Rowan never stopped clearing stone.

"Then we reach him first."

"We've been digging for six hours."

"Then we dig seven."

Cassian said nothing.

He simply lifted another slab with his good arm until the muscles trembled violently.

Malik's damaged leg gave out beneath him.

He stood again.

Lyra's hands bled openly.

She never noticed.

No one left.

Not one.

...

Late into the night...

A faint knock echoed from somewhere beneath the mountain.

Everyone froze.

Cassian dropped to one knee.

"Aidan!"

Silence.

Then...

Another weak knock.

Further away than before.

The mountain had shifted.

"He moved," Elias whispered.

"No."

Rowan looked toward the unstable rock overhead.

"The mountain moved."

Hope flickered.

Only for an instant.

The ceiling groaned.

A fresh shower of stone crashed into the rescue tunnel.

Everyone was forced backward.

Another path sealed itself.

Another hour was lost.

Cassian slammed his fist into the rubble.

For the first time in years...

He looked utterly helpless.

---

# Chapter Seventy-One: The Road That Ends

Dawn broke over East Hollow.

The rescued civilians gathered in silence near the ruined gorge.

No one celebrated surviving.

Too many people had watched the Crimson Witch disappear beneath the mountain to mistake survival for victory.

A little boy quietly walked toward Rowan.

It was the same child Aidan had nodded toward before releasing the Law.

The boy held something tightly in both hands.

"I found this."

Rowan looked down.

Aidan's crimson cloak clasp.

Bent.

Scratched.

Covered in dust.

He accepted it carefully.

Without a word.

...

By midday...

The rescuers finally broke through into a narrow pocket beneath the collapse.

Cassian crawled through first.

"Aidan!"

No answer.

Only slow breathing.

He found him sitting against a broken pillar of stone.

Half-buried.

Eyes closed.

Still conscious.

Barely.

Cassian knelt immediately.

"We've got you."

Aidan opened his eyes.

For a moment...

He didn't seem to recognize where he was.

Then his gaze settled on Cassian.

"The people?"

"They made it."

Aidan nodded once.

That was enough.

He tried to stand.

His legs failed immediately.

Cassian caught him before he fell.

"You don't have to prove anything."

Aidan looked toward the narrow shaft of daylight above them.

"I wasn't."

His voice had become quieter than ever.

"I was trying to walk."

Together, they slowly freed him from the surrounding stone.

Every movement drew another sharp breath from Aidan.

His body shook uncontrollably.

Not from fear.

From exhaustion.

When they finally reached the surface...

The rescued villagers stood waiting.

No cheers greeted him.

Only lowered heads.

A path quietly opened through the crowd.

People understood.

The man walking between them had paid for their lives with pieces of his own.

A little girl stepped forward.

She couldn't have been older than eight.

She held out a small wildflower.

Aidan looked at it for several seconds before accepting it.

"Thank you," she whispered.

He wanted to tell her she didn't owe him thanks.

He wanted to tell her to grow up somewhere peaceful.

He wanted to tell her the world could still become kinder.

Instead...

He simply gave the smallest nod.

There were no words left that felt large enough.

As the column began the slow journey back toward Blackwood Academy...

Aidan walked at its center.

Not because he was strongest.

Because he was the one everyone instinctively gathered around.

Each step cost him.

Each breath hurt.

His body had begun to fail him in ways even Helena could no longer hide.

Yet he kept walking.

Not toward victory.

Not toward hope.

Only toward the next place someone might still need him.

And somewhere, beyond the mountains and beyond his sight...

The road carrying Aidan Darkheart toward his final day as a human grew shorter still.

# Chapter Seventy-Two: The Last Road

Blackwood Academy did not have time to mourn.

By sunset, another rider reached the gates.

He did not carry a plea.

He carried a warning.

"The northern shelters..."

His voice shook.

"...they won't survive the night."

Rowan closed his eyes for only a moment.

Then he opened them again.

"We move."

No one objected.

Not even Helena.

She simply looked at Aidan.

His face had lost what little color remained.

His left arm stayed bound beneath his cloak.

Every breath lifted only one side of his chest.

His side bled through another layer of fresh bandages.

He looked as though the mountain had never released him.

Still...

He picked up his staff.

"I'm coming."

Helena almost spoke.

Then stopped.

She already knew.

Nothing she said would outweigh another life waiting to be saved.

...

The northern shelters stood against a narrow pass between two cliffs.

Hundreds of refugees crowded behind hastily raised barricades.

Children.

The elderly.

Families who had already fled more than once.

The moment they saw Blackwood arrive...

Relief spread across exhausted faces.

Not because an army had come.

Because Aidan had.

He noticed.

The weight settled deeper inside him.

They still believed.

...

The first impact shattered the outer barricade.

Stone exploded across the pass.

The defenders rushed forward.

Aidan walked past them.

"Stay behind me."

Only four words.

No speech.

No promise.

Only a command.

Cassian looked at him.

"You can barely stand."

"I know."

"I'll take the front."

Aidan shook his head.

"No."

His voice remained calm.

"They need time."

He stepped into the narrowest part of the pass.

Both hands rose.

Slowly.

Pain shook every muscle before he even cast.

"Hold."

Crimson light spread across fractured stone.

The cliff answered.

Weakly.

Cracks continued crawling beneath the Law.

Aidan pushed harder.

Blood spilled freely from his nose.

Then his mouth.

His vision dimmed.

The Law flickered.

Not because his will had weakened.

Because his body had.

Behind him...

Families ran.

Cassian, Lyra, Malik, Elias, Rowan, and Helena guided them through the narrowing escape.

No one looked away from Aidan.

They all saw it.

His hands shaking.

His knees failing.

His breathing breaking apart.

The Crimson Witch had reached the end of what flesh could bear.

...

The cliff exploded.

Aidan's Law collapsed beneath the weight.

Stone crashed through the barrier.

The shockwave hurled him backward across broken ground.

His staff flew from his hand.

He struck the earth hard enough that he didn't immediately rise.

Cassian reached him first.

"Aidan!"

Aidan opened his eyes.

Slowly.

"The people?"

"They're still moving."

Aidan nodded once.

"Good."

He tried to stand.

His legs folded beneath him.

Cassian caught him again.

"You can't."

"I have to."

"No."

"I have to."

He forced himself upright anyway.

Every step became an act of will.

He walked back toward the collapsing pass.

Not because he believed he could stop it.

Because someone still hadn't crossed.

A mother.

Holding a little girl.

Pinned beneath fallen timber.

Without hesitation...

Aidan reached them.

His injured shoulder screamed as he lifted the beam.

His ribs shifted beneath torn flesh.

The wound along his side burst completely open.

Warm blood poured onto the stone.

"Go."

The woman hesitated.

"Go."

She ran.

The little girl looked back only once.

Aidan gave the smallest nod.

Then turned back toward the mountain.

Another section broke loose.

Cassian saw it first.

His voice tore across the battlefield.

"Aidan!"

Too late.

The cliff came down.

Dust swallowed everything.

When it cleared...

Aidan still stood.

Barely.

Holding enough of the collapse back with his body and failing Law for the last refugees to escape.

The crimson light around his hands flickered like a dying flame.

Rowan stared in silent horror.

Helena's hands trembled.

Malik lowered his weapon.

Lyra covered her mouth.

Cassian took one step forward.

Then another.

The realization settled over every one of them at the same time.

This could not happen again.

There was nothing left for Aidan's body to give.

And yet...

He gave it anyway.
